### F-147: the delivery gap Phase 5 flagged — the desktop now reports what its sessions are doing, on change (2026-08-05, master) — RESOLVED

F-144's flagged item (a): read-session-state shipped as a server READ + MCP op with NO writer, so `dopl_channel(op="read_sessions")` answered an honest empty ("no live sessions being reported") on a machine that was working. Samuel asked for the capability explicitly — "what is flint doing?" from Claude Desktop. Architecture rules now in **ENGINEERING §18 "PHASE 5's DELIVERY GAP CLOSED"**.

**THE TWO BLOCKERS F-144 NAMED, AND WHAT EACH ONE ACTUALLY WANTED.**

1. **`session-summary.js` is deliberately network-free, and it stays that way.** It is the ONE place engine state becomes a pill state (F-142), it is import-free below its sentinel, and every one of its tests reads it as SOURCE and evaluates that block with two fakes injected — an `apiFetch` in it ends all three properties at once. So the writer is a SEPARATE module that SUBSCRIBES. The trigger was already there and was not re-invented: `flush()` coalesces a burst of engine dispatches at 200 ms and compares a digest, so it fires only when the projection really moved. It now emits a change event beside the renderer frame. **The two gates are separate on purpose** — `start()` resets the WINDOW's digest so a rebuilt renderer is repainted, and a window rebuild must not cost a server write, so the change digest is its own and is never reset. Pinned by "a REBUILT renderer repaints but is NOT a change".
2. **The summary shape lacked the workspace and the session key, and both were already in the module's hand.** `channel_sessions` keys on `(user_id, session_key)` and fences on `workspace_id`. `sessionId` is the wrong key — a park, a lazy resume or a crash recreate mints a fresh one for the same session — and the STABLE key is `s.key`, which this module already keys its name ledger by; `s.workspaceId` rides the same session object. So `reportList()` is `list()`'s one pass carrying two more fields and `list()` narrows them back off: **the IPC payload and `DesktopSessionSummary` are byte-unchanged**, one derivation with two projections. `noteEnded` freezes `workspaceId` alongside the rest of the identity, because a retained ended entry has left the registry and there is nowhere else left to read it from. Every other column comes from where it honestly lives: `name` and `state` are `session-summary`'s own (the writer never re-derives a state — a source test asserts it does not so much as mention `phase` or `activity`), and `channel_name` / `thread_title` are `displayText`-neutralized already, which is what the migration's F-145 comment assumed of a writer that did not exist yet.

**THE ROW LIFETIME, decided and stated rather than left to a future reader.** An `ended` session's row is **DELETED**, not kept and swept. The rule is F-142's, inherited: a pill lives exactly as long as its window, the row IS that projection, so the row lives exactly as long as the pill. Kept-plus-sweep loses twice — a sweep needs a scheduler this product does not have, so "later" means "never" and the table grows without bound (the accumulation plan §5 exists to shed), and an `ended` row with no window answers "what is flint doing?" with a session the operator cannot open, when the channel transcript already carries what happened. **The delete is implicit**, which is why each push carries the whole set for a workspace and the server replaces it: a delta protocol needs a removal message a crashed desktop never sends. Process death is bounded by a per-operator persisted record (`sessionReportWorkspaces`, `userId -> [workspaceId]`), whose one job is the run that starts with no sessions in a workspace a previous run left rows in — one empty push, then forgotten. **Signing out is NOT covered and is stated as such**: the credential that could delete the rows is gone before anything can react, so they stand until that account signs in on this Mac again.

**THE CROSS-ACCOUNT GUARD, which is the part with prior form.** Signing out does not end sessions — the engine owns their lifetime and `shell-mode.js` says so — so operator A's sessions are still projecting when B signs in on the same Mac. A push under B's credential would file A's handles, channel names and thread titles as B's, and B can read them: that is a third cross-account bug in this project's own idiom. The guard is an ORIGIN STAMP — every session key is stamped with the identity current when the writer first SAW it, and only keys stamped with the identity being posted as are ever reported. **It fails closed**: a key first seen with no resolvable identity is never reported, because adopting it is precisely the leak (the writer cannot tell an unattributed session from another operator's). A sign-out and a sign-in as the SAME operator resumes on its own, since the stamp matches again. The stamp ledger is pruned to the live set on every cycle, so it is bounded by the window budget like everything else.

**THE FAILURE POSTURE.** Two attempts (one retry at 2 s, only for a throw / 5xx / 429 — a 4xx will not answer differently), then stop. **A failed push records no digest, so the retry cadence is the session's own state changes** — bounded by the session's life, with no timer anywhere, which is the shape `ui-sync`'s ~39 000-attempt storm is the cautionary tale for. One log line per `(workspace, failure shape)`, and it says what it COSTS rather than only that it happened (the 1.8.x outage was invisible for hours because the failing branch logged nothing actionable); a success clears the latch so a later outage is reported again. A cycle in flight coalesces the newest projection rather than running a second one in parallel.

**ON THE SERVER, THE ONE THING THAT IS NOT OBVIOUS: the write reads first.** `replaceSessionStates` SELECTs the caller's current rows, upserts only those that DIFFER, and deletes by explicit key. `updated_at` is the read's `ORDER BY` and the "when did this session last move" the MCP result reports, and the trigger stamps it on every UPDATE (F-145) — so an unconditional whole-set upsert would touch every row on every push and five sessions would all claim to have changed when one did. Plausible, wrong, and the exact failure shape the migration's own `updated_at` note describes. The comparison is field-by-field and deliberately not `JSON.stringify`: key order differs between a PostgREST row and a service-built object, and a string compare would report every row as changed, which is the same defect wearing a different hat.

**WHAT WAS DELIBERATELY NOT DONE.** (a) **No credential bound on the write.** The rows are the caller's own and only the caller can read them, so narrowing to a runtime stamp or a cookie session would refuse a future desktop lane and buy no boundary — F-145's own warning about `desktop-session`. (b) **The write does not degrade `PGRST205`.** The READ can honestly answer `[]` over a missing relation; a WRITE that swallowed it would report a store that did not happen, so until the migration lands the POST throws, the route answers 500 and the desktop says so once per workspace per run — which is the true state of the world, said once. F-145's read degrade is untouched and still tested. (c) **`agent_presence` is still untouched** — sequencing item 7 says MEASURE, and this store is now the thing to measure against. (d) **No heartbeat, no timer, no realtime publication** on the new table (the migration's own "not published to realtime" note stands: the web pills read the desktop over IPC).

**THE RESIDUE SWEEP, because F-146's lesson is five days old.** Six places said the write "is not wired in this phase" — `repository-sessions`' docblock, `session-state-service`, the route header, `channel-ops-read.ts` twice, `parity.test.ts` — and every one of them would have been a confident wrong answer the moment this landed. All corrected in place with the annotation style this tree uses (strike the claim, say what replaced it). One unrelated stale copy was corrected in passing: `types.ts#SessionPillState` still blamed streaming for the absent `thinking` state, which F-146 corrected in four other places and missed here.
